[PATCH v2 2/2] KVM: selftests: Test that an NMI-window single-step preserves the guest's RF
Author: Peng Hao <flyingpenghao@gmail.com>

Extend the guest-handled part of debug_regs with the case where KVM
single-steps the IRET of an NMI handler to find out when NMIs are
unblocked (SVM without vNMI), and that IRET returns to an instruction
breakpoint with RF set in the popped RFLAGS.

The guest arms an instruction breakpoint, sends itself an NMI that is
delivered at the breakpoint address, and from the NMI handler sends a
second NMI, which stays pending, and sets RF in the interrupt frame.
The second NMI is delivered once the first handler's IRET completes,
and when it returns the breakpoint must not fire.

Without the preceding fix this fails on AMD with kvm_amd.vnmi=0, as the
guest takes a #DB at the breakpoint.  It passes with vNMI, and on Intel,
where KVM doesn't single-step to open the NMI window.

Signed-off-by: Peng Hao <flyingpeng@tencent.com>
---

[PATCH 5.10.y] KVM: x86: Acquire kvm->srcu when handling KVM_SET_VCPU_EVENTS
Author: Dickson Wu <dickson.wu@certik.com>


commit 4bcdd831d9d01e0fb64faea50732b59b2ee88da1 upstream.

Grab kvm->srcu when processing KVM_SET_VCPU_EVENTS, as KVM will forcibly
leave nested VMX/SVM if SMM mode is being toggled, and leaving nested VMX
reads guest memory.

Note, kvm_vcpu_ioctl_x86_set_vcpu_events() can also be called from KVM_RUN
via sync_regs(), which already holds SRCU.  I.e. trying to precisely use
kvm_vcpu_srcu_read_lock() around the problematic SMM code would cause
problems.  Acquiring SRCU isn't all that expensive, so for simplicity,
grab it unconditionally for KVM_SET_VCPU_EVENTS.

This is the 5.15.y backport (5f35099fa3d5 in linux-5.15.y), which
applies to 5.10.y unchanged. 5.15.y and newer already have it.

Tested on v5.10.275:
- x86_64 allmodconfig W=1 build of arch/x86/kvm/x86.o, base vs patched,
  no new warnings
- KVM-enabled defconfig boots under QEMU/KVM, and the existing
  evmcs_test and smm_test KVM selftests pass on it

One thing I noticed while there: in 5.10 (and 5.15), KVM_RUN calls
sync_regs() before taking kvm->srcu, so that path can still reach
kvm_vcpu_ioctl_x86_set_vcpu_events() without it. The upstream message
says sync_regs() already holds SRCU, which is true in mainline but not
in these branches. I left it alone to keep this identical to 5.15.y.

The backport and the test runs were prepared with AI coding tools
(OpenAI Codex agents, with review by Claude). I went over the result
before signing off.

[PATCH 2/3] KVM: VMX: Treat partial support of an entry/exit pair as inconsistent
Author: Sohil Mehta <sohil.mehta@intel.com>

The VM-entry/VM-exit pair check only requires one of a pair's exit
controls to be supported along with the entry control. That is fine
while every pair has a single exit control.

However, a feature can have multiple exit controls paired with a single
entry control. For example, PAT and EFER each have two exit controls,
but KVM doesn't use their save controls. In future, FRED will use both:
its state is loaded on VM-entry, and both saved and restored on VM-exit.

If hardware supports only one of those exit controls, the existing check
passes and the feature would be enabled with part of its state left
unswitched. Require all of a pair's controls to be supported, or none of
them. The existing pairs are unaffected.

Signed-off-by: Sohil Mehta <sohil.mehta@intel.com>
---
v1:
 - New patch. It addresses a gap Sashiko found in v10 patch 2:
   https://lore.kernel.org/all/20260911220046.1A4A91F000FF@smtp.kernel.org/
 - Doesn't include a fixes tag because all existing pairs have a single
   exit control.
---

[PATCH 1/3] KVM: VMX: Add support for the secondary VM-exit controls
Author: Sohil Mehta <sohil.mehta@intel.com>


The secondary VM-exit controls are a 64-bit VMCS field for controls that
no longer fit in the 32-bit primary field. Bit 31 of the primary VM-exit
controls activates them. No feature KVM supports uses any of these
controls yet, but the upcoming FRED KVM support is planning to use them.

Add the VMCS field, its 64-bit controls shadow and the vmcs_config
plumbing, and activate the controls in vmcs01 whenever hardware supports
them. With every control bit clear, KVM behaves exactly as before.

Check the secondary controls through their own entry/exit pair table.
Avoid using a shared table because a failed check would clear the
activation bit and turn off every secondary control, not just the
inconsistent one. The new table stays empty until the first feature adds
a pair.

Copy the controls into vmcs02 as well. vmcs02 inherits the activation
bit from vmcs01, so without the copy it would run with the secondary
controls active but empty. That is harmless while every bit is clear.
Once a feature sets one, though, L2 would run with it silently off,
leaving the host with L2's state after a VM exit.

Signed-off-by: Xin Li (Intel) <xin@zytor.com>
Co-developed-by: Sohil Mehta <sohil.mehta@intel.com>
Signed-off-by: Sohil Mehta <sohil.mehta@intel.com>
Reviewed-by: Binbin Wu <binbin.wu@linux.intel.com>
---
v1:
(Changes from FRED KVM v10):
 - Set the secondary VM-exit controls in vmcs02 too, so that no commit
   leaves vmcs02 with the controls activated but empty. (Prevents a CPU
   hang on exits from L2 once FRED guest support lands without the
   nested patches.)
 - Match the names and placement of the new variables and code to their
   neighbours.
 - Reword the changelog and also say why the secondary entry/exit pair
   table is separate from the primary one.
---

[PATCH 0/3] KVM: VMX: Add support for the secondary VM-exit controls
Author: Sohil Mehta <sohil.mehta@intel.com>

Sean, Paolo,

These patches started out as part of the FRED KVM series [1]. However, the
series is getting bulky (30+ patches) and hard to manage as well as review. I
am working on splitting it into smaller parts [2] for easier review and merge.

The overall goal is to get the infrastructure pieces ready first and then the
FRED guest support followed by the nested FRED support. Some core x86 patches
that were part of the FRED KVM series are already in place [3] with more on
the way [4].

This series focusses on getting the secondary VM-exit controls support in
place independent of FRED. Please let me know if you prefer a different
approach or have any suggestions.

Background
----------
The secondary VM-exit controls are a 64-bit VMCS field, activated by bit 31 of
the primary VM-exit controls. The SDM defines four of them so far: "save
FRED", "load FRED", "load IA32_SPEC_CTRL" and "prematurely busy shadow stack".

With this series, KVM discovers the field and programs it in vmcs01 and
vmcs02, so that FRED or any other user of these controls only has to set its
own bits later on.

Patches
-------
Patch 1 adds the support for the secondary VM-exit controls.

Changes from FRED KVM v10:
 - Reword the commit message and add some minor changes to match the name
   and placement with neighboring code.
 - Copy the secondary controls into vmcs02 to fix the issue described below.
   In v10 this lived in the nested patches [5] which tied the FRED guest
   support to the nested support.

Binbin, I have kept your review tag since the changes are minor. Please let me
know if you have any concerns.

Patch 2 fixes an issue reported by sashiko related to the entry/exit pair
checking [6] and patch 3 adds the new field to the VMCS dump. The last two
patches are optional, but they help complete the support.

The patches are based on the kvm-x86/next branch (kvm-x86-next-2026.11.11).

vmcs02 issue
------------
KVM seeds vmcs02's VM-exit controls from vmcs01's, so vmcs02 inherits bit
31 but not the secondary controls themselves. vmcs02 then runs with the
secondary controls active but empty, which is harmless only while every
bit is clear.

Once FRED sets its controls in vmcs01, vmcs02 also inherits FRED's VM-entry
control. Entering any L2 loads FRED state, but the exit from L2 doesn't
restore the host's. Until the next VM entry the host runs with a zeroed FRED
configuration, and any event in that window hangs the CPU. 

This isn't specific to FRED, any future secondary VM-exit control would
likewise be silently off whenever an L2 runs.

Testing
-------
I tested it on a CWF system with FRED enabled as well as fred=off. The CPU
enumerates all four secondary VM-exit controls (IA32_VMX_EXIT_CTLS2 = 0xf), so
KVM activates the field in vmcs01 and copies it into vmcs02, with every bit
clear.

A debug dump_vmcs() on first VM exit from vmcs01 and from vmcs02 shows the
same controls in both:
    ExitControls=0x902befff SecondaryExitControls=0x0000000000000000

KVM selftests and kvm-unit-tests show no regressions.

Links
-----

[2]: https://lore.kernel.org/lkml/12f686a4-a9f8-4347-a7f4-c0f893addfb9@intel.com/
[3]: https://lore.kernel.org/lkml/179545148741.92412.5844266518161867015.tip-bot2@tip-bot2/
[4]: https://lore.kernel.org/lkml/c662df85-4431-4303-9865-4189d186a94c@intel.com/

[PATCH v2] RISC-V: KVM: Account G-stage page table memory
Author: Tan Chi <tanchi25@mails.ucas.ac.cn>

RISC-V KVM currently does not account G-stage page table memory
in NR_SECONDARY_PAGETABLE.

Use kvm_account_pgtable_pages() to account for the allocation and
freeing of the root and lower-level page tables, including those
allocated when splitting huge page mappings.

This makes G-stage page table memory usage visible through
SecPageTables in /proc/meminfo and sec_pagetables in the cgroup
memory.stat interface.

Update the documentation accordingly.

Tested on a RISC-V KVM host running under QEMU TCG with Sv57x4.
The observed G-stage page table memory usage was:

  100 empty VMs:                         1600 KiB
  8 GiB sparsely mapped with 4 KiB pages: 16448 KiB
  256 MiB mapped with 2 MiB pages:          36 KiB
  After enabling dirty logging:           548 KiB

The accounting in /proc/meminfo, /proc/vmstat and cgroup
memory.stat matched the expected values and returned to zero
after destroying the VMs.

This reimplements the work originally proposed by Yosry Ahmed
in 2022, adapted to the current G-stage MMU code.

Link: https://lore.kernel.org/r/20220426053904.3684293-6-yosryahmed@google.com

Signed-off-by: Tan Chi <tanchi25@mails.ucas.ac.cn>
---
Changes in v2:
- Use the allocation order to calculate the number of root page
  table pages, fixing a Clang warning reported by the kernel test
  robot.
  https://lore.kernel.org/oe-kbuild-all/20261126015340.s7bzBtEB-lkp@intel.com/

[kvm-unit-tests PATCH 3/3] x86/pmu: Check emulated instruction/branch count difference on AMD
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

On AMD, the emulated instruction test can only check that the
instructions and branches counts are at least the expected counts, as
asynchronous VM-Exits, e.g. due to host IRQs, add to both counts (AMD
counts a successful VMRUN as a retired instruction and branch in guest
mode).  This doesn't catch KVM overcounting emulated instructions or
branches, e.g. if KVM retired an emulated non-branch instruction twice,
or counted it as a branch.

But as every VMRUN adds one to both counts, the difference between the
counts is precise.  When GLOBAL_CTRL is supported, i.e. when the
counters are enabled and disabled by the same WRMSRs, check that the
difference between the counts is the expected one, to catch KVM over- or
undercounting emulated instructions or branches unevenly.  It can't
catch KVM overcounting both events by the same amount, but the presets
and the ">=" checks already catch undercounting.

Only do this on AMD: on Intel without the overcount errata, the precise
checks already verify both counts, and with the errata the overcount
isn't guaranteed to be the same for both events (e.g. only instructions
are overcounted on some CPUs).

Signed-off-by: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>
---
 x86/pmu.c | 13 +++++++++++++
 1 file changed, 13 insertions(+)

----------------------------------------------------------------------

[kvm-unit-tests PATCH 2/3] x86/pmu: Account for VMRUNs in emulated instruction checks on AMD
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

AMD CPUs count a successful VMRUN as a retired instruction and as a
retired branch in guest mode, i.e. every VM-Exit taken in the measured
window of check_emulated_instr() adds one to both counts, including the
10 VM-Exits for the 5 forced emulations and the 5 CPUIDs themselves.
E.g. on AMD Turin, the counts are 32 instructions (22 expected) and 15
branches (5 expected) with a mediated PMU, and 33/16 with KVM's
perf-based vPMU, which also intercepts the GLOBAL_CTRL write that
enables the counters.  KVM's accounting of the emulated instructions is
exact, the excess is all VMRUNs.

Since commit 7999ff1d28b0 ("x86/pmu: Treat all AMD CPUs as having
instruction/branch overcount errata"), the test only checks that the
counts are at least the expected counts on AMD, which always passes: the
measured window has 12 native instructions and no native branches, so
the 10 VMRUNs alone yield 22 instructions and 10 branches, enough to
pass ">= 22" and ">= 5" and to overflow the counters from the -22/-5
presets, even if KVM didn't account for any of the emulated
instructions.

On AMD, add the 10 synchronous VMRUNs to the expected counts and to the
presets, so that the counters only reach the expected counts, and only
overflow, if KVM retired all of the emulated instructions and branches.

Fixes: 7999ff1d28b0 ("x86/pmu: Treat all AMD CPUs as having instruction/branch overcount errata")
Signed-off-by: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>
---
 x86/pmu.c | 33 +++++++++++++++++++++++++++------
 1 file changed, 27 insertions(+), 6 deletions(-)

[kvm-unit-tests PATCH 1/3] x86/pmu: Mask emulated instruction count deltas to the counter width
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

check_emulated_instr() presets each counter to -N, N being the expected
number of instructions or branches, and computes the number of counted
events as "count - start", where start is -N as a 64-bit value.

That only works if the counter wraps.  The counter is only
gp_counter_width bits wide, so if it counts fewer than N events, it
reads back as a large positive value (2^width - N + delta), and the
64-bit subtraction yields 2^width + delta instead of delta.  E.g. a
48-bit counter preset to -22 that counts only 10 events reads back as
0xfffffffffff4, and the difference is 0x100000000000a instead of 10.

The "==" checks still fail in this case, but the ">=" checks, used
without GLOBAL_CTRL and on CPUs with the instructions/branches retired
overcount errata (i.e. all AMD CPUs), pass even though the counter
didn't count the expected number of events.

Mask the differences to the counter width, so that a counter that didn't
count the expected number of events fails the ">=" checks.

Fixes: 20cf91472daa ("x86/pmu: Test PMU virtualization on emulated instructions")
Signed-off-by: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>
---
 x86/pmu.c | 13 ++++++++-----
 1 file changed, 8 insertions(+), 5 deletions(-)

[kvm-unit-tests PATCH 0/3] x86/pmu: Restore emulated instruction test coverage on AMD
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

Since commit 7999ff1d28b0 ("x86/pmu: Treat all AMD CPUs as having
instruction/branch overcount errata"), check_emulated_instr() only
checks that the instructions and branches counts are at least the
expected counts on AMD. That always passes, because AMD counts each
VMRUN as a retired instruction and branch in guest mode, and the 10
synchronous VM-Exits in the measured window alone are enough to reach
the expected counts (and overflow the counters), even if KVM doesn't
account for any of the emulated instructions.

Patch 1 fixes a false pass in the ">=" checks when a counter doesn't
wrap, which predates the errata change but now affects all AMD CPUs.

Patch 2 accounts for the synchronous VMRUNs in the expected counts and
presets on AMD, so that the checks fail if KVM undercounts emulated
instructions.

Patch 3 adds a check that the difference between the instructions and
branches counts is exact on AMD. Every VMRUN adds one to both counts,
so the difference is precise regardless of asynchronous VM-Exits, which
catches KVM over- or undercounting instructions vs. branches unevenly.

Tested on AMD Turin with KVM's mediated and perf-based vPMUs, and on
Intel Granite Rapids, with and without injected (simulated) KVM
miscounting to verify that the new checks catch it. With the perf-based
vPMU on AMD, "emulated instruction: branch counter overflow" fails with
and without this series, due to a KVM/perf issue with periods of 1:
https://lore.kernel.org/all/20231107183605.409588-1-seanjc@google.com/

Yosry Ahmed (3):
  x86/pmu: Mask emulated instruction count deltas to the counter width
  x86/pmu: Account for VMRUNs in emulated instruction checks on AMD
  x86/pmu: Check emulated instruction/branch count difference on AMD

[PATCH 2/3] KVM: x86/pmu: Always defer PMU reprogramming on SVM nested transitions
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

Always defer PMU counter reprogramming via KVM_REQ_PMU on SVM nested
transitions and EFER.SVME toggles instead of invoking
kvm_pmu_handle_event() synchronously. The motivation for synchronous
handling was to ensure to-be-emulated PMCs are updated before
instruction emulation completes, such that instructions count in the
mode they retire in (as per the hardware behavior).

However, since kvm_pmu_request_counters_reprogram() now recalculates
to-be-emulated PMCs synchronously, and kvm_pmu_recalc_pmc_emulation() ->
pmc_is_locally_enabled() already accounts for Host-Only and Guest-Only
bits on AMD, VMRUN or WRMSR(MSR_EFER) would correctly be counted in the
mode they retire in.

In addition to being a nice cleanup, this fixes an unprotected SRCU
access (and potential UAF) when userspace updates EFER.SVME via
KVM_SET_SREGS[2], as svm_set_efer() ends up calling
kvm_mediated_pmu_refresh_event_filter() -> pmc_is_event_allowed(), which
dereferences kvm->arch.pmu_event_filter via srcu_dereference(). This
results in the following splat (with CONFIG_PROVE_RCU):

  WARNING: suspicious RCU usage
  arch/x86/kvm/pmu.c:539 suspicious rcu_dereference_check() usage!
  ...
  pmc_is_event_allowed+0x27c/0x2b0 [kvm]
  kvm_pmu_handle_event+0x365/0x880 [kvm]
  svm_set_efer+0x16b/0x1a0 [kvm_amd]

[PATCH 1/3] KVM: x86/pmu: Recalc PMC emulation when deferring counter reprogramming
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

In preparation for always deferring counter reprogramming, recalculate
PMC emulation for each counter first. On AMD, this would make sure that
mode changes (e.g. via VMRUN or toggling EFER.SVME) are immediately
propagated before instruction emulation completes, such that
instructions are counted in the mode they retire in as hardware does.

This gives kvm_pmu_request_counters_reprogram() similar treatment to
kvm_pmu_request_counter_reprogram(), as done in commit 5dfd498bad5f
("KVM: x86/pmu: Calculate set of to-be-emulated PMCs at time of
WRMSRs").

Now that kvm_pmu_request_counter_reprogram() is just a single-counter
version of kvm_pmu_request_counters_reprogram(), just call the latter
from the former.

No functional change intended, as all architectural mode-changing paths
on AMD do not currently defer counter reprogramming.

Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/pmu.h | 26 ++++++++++++++++----------
 1 file changed, 16 insertions(+), 10 deletions(-)

[PATCH 0/3] KVM: x86/pmu: Always defer PMU reprogramming on nested transitions
Author: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>

When Host-Only/Guest-Only bits handling was added [1], we went back and
forth between deferring PMU reprogramming vs doing it synchronously.

Deferring all reprogramming is incorrect, because on mode changes (e.g.
VMRUN or WRMSR(EFER.SVME)), this would only update the to-be-emulated
PMCs after the instruction is emulated, which would count the
instruction in the previous mode. Real AMD hardware counts instructions
in the mode they retire in.

We ended up with a hybrid approach where architectural mode changes
do reprogramming synchronously, but force-leaving nested defers it, as
it may not be safe to do it synchronously.

This mini-series unifies all nested transition paths by always
calculating to-be-emulated PMCs synchronously and deferring the rest
(e.g. similar to WRMSR(EVENTSEL)).

The motivation for the series was an unprotected SRCU access from
KVM_SET_SREGS -> svm_set_efer -> svm_pmu_handle_nested_transition() ->
... -> pmc_is_event_allowed(). The obvious/simple fix (and probably more
stable-friendly one) is to hold the SRCU lock in kvm_pmu_handle_event()
when reprogramming the counters. However, I think this is a nice cleanup
to have regardless of the SRCU bug.

[1] https://lore.kernel.org/kvm/20260527234711.4175166-1-yosry@kernel.org/

Yosry Ahmed (3):
  KVM: x86/pmu: Recalc PMC emulation when deferring counter
    reprogramming
  KVM: x86/pmu: Always defer PMU reprogramming on SVM nested transitions
  KVM: selftests: Set EFER.SVME via KVM_SET_SREGS in
    svm_pmu_host_guest_test

 arch/x86/kvm/pmu.c                            |  1 -

[PATCH v2 2/2] KVM: s390: selftests: Add a test for nested guests in ESA mode
Author: Yvan Janssens <yvanj@cyber.gent>

Run a nested guest from a state description without the z/Architecture
flag, with KVM_CAP_S390_VSIE_ESAMODE enabled, and let it execute STORE
FACILITY LIST EXTENDED twice. vsie intercepts the first one of every SIE
entry to shadow the facility list guest 2 designates and then has the
instruction repeated.

The test checks that the second doubleword of the PSW field, which is not
part of an ESA/390 PSW, comes back unchanged, that the PSW points behind
the last instruction, and that the first instruction stored the
designated list. What the second instruction stored is printed for
diagnosis.

The test is skipped without the capability, without interpretive
execution for the guest (SIEF2) and without facility 7 in the guest's
model.

Signed-off-by: Yvan Janssens <yvanj@cyber.gent>
---

 .../selftests/kvm/s390/vsie_esa_test.c        | 180 ++++++++++++++++++
 2 files changed, 181 insertions(+)

[PATCH v2 1/2] KVM: s390: vsie: Rewind the PSW of ESA mode guests in the right format
Author: Yvan Janssens <yvanj@cyber.gent>

ESA-mode guests in vsie have existed since commit b0ad874d9852 ("KVM:
s390: vsie: Allow non-zarch guests"); they use the 8-byte short PSW -
the mask and instruction address share one doubleword.

vsie's PSW rewind originally still changed the second doubleword, which
is unused in ESA mode, so the instruction is skipped instead of
repeated. The visible effect is that the first STFLE after entering SIE
does nothing in a nested guest.

This patch adds a vsie-specific rewind function that handles both
formats; no short-PSW type is introduced - for ESA guests mask and
address are written into the mask field.

Fixes: b0ad874d9852 ("KVM: s390: vsie: Allow non-zarch guests")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org # 7.1+
Signed-off-by: Yvan Janssens <yvanj@cyber.gent>
---
 arch/s390/kvm/s390/vsie.c | 18 ++++++++++++++++--
 1 file changed, 16 insertions(+), 2 deletions(-)

[PATCH v2 0/2] KVM: s390: vsie: fix instruction retry for nested guests in ESA mode
Author: Yvan Janssens <yvanj@cyber.gent>

This series of patches fixes vsie rewinding the PSW of ESA-mode nested
guests in the wrong doubleword, which made an intercepted instruction be
skipped instead of repeated. Patch 1 is the fix, patch 2 is a selftest
that reproduces the issue. The base is the current mainline
(v7.4-rc4-260).

Claude has been used to try to find this bug; it was used to figure out
what went wrong and what would be the most appropriate way to fix the
problem. I've reviewed, verified and tested the results myself on my own
machine (MTM 3907).

Changes in v2:
- patch 1: shorter commit message;
- patch 1: removed the comment block above vsie_rewind_psw(), added a
  short comment in the ESA branch (as requested by Janosch);
- dropped the Assisted-by trailers (removed from the documentation in
  the 7.4 cycle); the AI assistance is disclosed above instead;
- no code change in patch 2.

v1: https://lore.kernel.org/all/20261125151607.3039477-1-yvanj@cyber.gent/

Also - a note on the context of this fix; these problems were identified
when trying to bring up Proxmox on s390x in my lab while adding support
for non-Linux guests. This bug was found while trying to bring up z/VM
as a KVM guest. There is another patch set on its way as an RFC to add
the ESA/390-compatibility mode for first-level KVM guests, for programs
that start in ESA/390 mode themselves, such as stand-alone utilities and
the early IPL code of older systems. z/VM's ESA guests, e.g. CMS,
already run as nested guests with this fix; what is still missing there
is support for its ESA/XC guests, which is later work.

I understand the concern wrt. ESA accommodations; the annoying thing is
that there are a bunch of potential guest OSes from the transition
period from ESA to z/Arch only that need this to work to survive their
initial bootstrap until they can reach z/Architecture mode; one example
here is the IPL text used in z/OS 1.10->1.12.

I also understand that the support cycle for these systems expired long
ago, and that the support cycle for a 3907 has also expired. My
remaining question here is if you are open to support for this, as long
as this does not provide a significant amount of overhead for
maintenance.

If you want to discuss this off-list, I'm also available on the Open
Mainframe Project Slack.

[PATCH 2/2] KVM: s390: Enable the generic vCPU kick infrastructure
Author: Steffen Eiden <seiden@linux.ibm.com>

Drop the #ifndef CONFIG_S390 around __kvm_vcpu_kick() and its inline
wrappers so that arm on s390 can use the vCPU kick infrastructure.

Commit 0266c894b51c ("KVM: put back #ifndef CONFIG_S390 around
kvm_vcpu_kick") added the guard around the kick itself for exactly one
reason: kvm_vcpu_kick() calls smp_send_reschedule(), which s390 did not
export, and so the s390 KVM module failed to link.

KVM for arm64 guests on s390 builds on the shared arm64 KVM code, which
relies on the generic kick infrastructure. s390 will have a user for the
kick infrastructure. Let's activate it.

Native KVM/s390 is unaffected by the guards going away. It keeps its own
wakeup and request paths and gains no callers of the generic kick.

Signed-off-by: Steffen Eiden <seiden@linux.ibm.com>
---

 virt/kvm/kvm_main.c      | 2 --
 2 files changed, 6 deletions(-)

[PATCH 0/2] KVM: s390: Enable vCPU kick infrastructure
Author: Steffen Eiden <seiden@linux.ibm.com>

Future extensions to arm on s390 will require the vcpu kick
infrastructure from KVM.

Today that infrastructure is compiled out on s390: Commit 0266c894b51c
("KVM: put back #ifndef CONFIG_S390 around kvm_vcpu_kick") guarded it
with #ifndef CONFIG_S390 for exactly one reason: kvm_vcpu_kick() calls
smp_send_reschedule(), which s390 did not export, and therefore the
s390 KVM module failed to link.

So Patch 1 exports arch_smp_send_reschedule and patch 2 drops the
CONFIG_S390 guards around __kvm_vcpu_kick() and its inline wrappers.

As this is a non-invasive change  and eases the integration of future
code I send it in beforehand. These two patches need to come in before
arm patches which add the sharing of code using this e.g.
vcpu_power_off, vcpu_suspend, ... which will be used in the near future.

Native KVM/s390 is unaffected. It keeps its own wakeup and request
paths and gains no callers of the generic kick. __kvm_vcpu_kick() is
now compiled into the native module but unused; it cannot send an IPI
for a native vCPU anyway, as native s390 never sets vcpu->mode to
IN_GUEST_MODE. Then kvm_request_needs_ipi() declines and the kick
would result in a plain wakeup of a halted vCPU.DITME: cover title for vcpu_kick

	Steffen

[PATCH v5 5/5] KVM: x86: nVMX: fix races in delivery of nested posted interrupts
Author: Maxim Levitsky <mlevitsk@redhat.com>

If a nested posted interrupt races with a L0 vm exit on a target vCPU,
that is reflected to L1, then this nestedf posted interrupt is currently
neither processed nor delivered to L1 as a regular interrupt.

Since it is not possible to know if APICv hardware has processed
the interrupt, opt to always process it a moment before nested VM exit.
(call vmx_complete_nested_posted_interrupt)

To ensure that vmx_deliver_nested_posted_interrupt and
vmx_complete_nested_posted_interrupt don't race do the following:

Have a cmpxchg based loop in vmx_deliver_nested_posted_interrupt that
atomically checks that target vCPU is in nested mode and has suitable
posted interrupt configuration, and then set KVM_NESTED_PI_PENDING.

If the above cmpxchg loop succeeds, this means that even if the target vCPU
is in process of exiting the nested guest, it will notice this
KVM_NESTED_PI_PENDING flag when it reaches its
vmx_complete_nested_posted_interrupt, and then it will process it.

If it fails, it means that the target vCPU is no longer in the guest
mode, and thus it is safe to deliver the interrupt as a normal interrupt.

Apply a similar logic on the receiving side:
Use xchg to snapshot the old value of guest state in leave_guest_mode,
and by using this snapshot, determine if we need to process
the posted interrupts.

Also delay publish to of the nested posted interrupt state to
KVM_REQ_GET_NESTED_STATE_PAGES to avoid dealing with a situation where
vm exit causes a L2->L2 vm exit before nested APICv pages were mapped.

Fixes: 705699a13994 ("KVM: nVMX: Enable nested posted interrupt processing")

Suggested-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
Signed-off-by: Maxim Levitsky <mlevitsk@redhat.com>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/regs.h       |  5 ++--
 arch/x86/kvm/vmx/nested.c | 63 ++++++++++++++++++++++++++-------------
 arch/x86/kvm/vmx/vmx.c    | 60 ++++++++++++++++++-------------------
 3 files changed, 76 insertions(+), 52 deletions(-)

[PATCH v5 1/5] KVM: nVMX: don't check PID.ON when processing nested posted interrupts
Author: Maxim Levitsky <mlevitsk@redhat.com>

Despite the suggestion to test and set the PID.ON and avoid sending
a posted interrupt if it is already set, there is no requirement
for this bit to be set for posted interrupt processing to happen
when the target CPU receives the posted notification vector.

See section 30.6 POSTED-INTERRUPT PROCESSING:

   "The processor clears the outstanding-notification bit in
    the posted-interrupt descriptor. This is done atomically so as to leave
    the remainder of the descriptor unmodified
    (e.g., with a locked AND operation)."

Apparently Windows' implementation of APICv does exactly this:
it sets bits in PIR, without bothering to also set the PID.ON.

Fixes: 705699a13994 ("KVM: nVMX: Enable nested posted interrupt processing")

Signed-off-by: Maxim Levitsky <mlevitsk@redhat.com>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/vmx/nested.c | 15 +++++++++++----
 1 file changed, 11 insertions(+), 4 deletions(-)

----------------------------------------------------------------------

[PATCH v5 4/5] KVM: x86: nVMX: move nested APICv state to nested_guest_state
Author: Maxim Levitsky <mlevitsk@redhat.com>

Replace vmx->nested.pi_pending,pi_active and posted_intr_nv with
KVM_NESTED_PI_VECTOR_MASK,KVM_NESTED_PI_ACTIVE,KVM_NESTED_PI_PENDING
flags of nested_guest_state.

Since the nested state is cleared as a whole to 0, on each nested VM exit,
explicit clearing of vmx->nested.posted_intr_nv and vmx->nested.pi_pending
was dropped.

Also, since nested entry sets nested_guest_state to KVM_NESTED_IN_GUEST,
the publishing of KVM_NESTED_PI_VECTOR_MASK,KVM_NESTED_PI_ACTIVE is delayed
to happen after enter_guest_mode().

[PATCH v5 0/5] KVM: nVMX: fix nested APICv emulation for windows guests
Author: Maxim Levitsky <mlevitsk@redhat.com>

Fix two problems that were recently discovered that cause nested APICv=0D
to malfunction on windows with hyperv enabled.=0D
=0D
The first problem is that unlike KVM, windows doesn't set PIR.ON when=0D
sending posted interrupts. X86 spec actually allows this.=0D
=0D
The second problem is that unlilke KVM, which mostly ignores them,=0D
windows depends on accurate delivery of the posted notification=0D
interrupts, and currently it is possible for a posted notification=0D
to get lost it it arrives at the same time as the nested VM exit.=0D
=0D
V2: to avoid spurious notification in L1, process the posted=0D
notification in L2, just before VM exit.=0D
=0D
V3: rework the patch series following the pseudocode that Paolo suggested=0D
(Thanks!!)=0D
=0D
V4: random fixes, including sashiko feedback.=0D
V5: restore defered error handling on PID map failure.=0D
=0D
Best regards,=0D
	Maxim Levitsky=0D

[PATCH v2 12/20] KVM: arm64: Advertise TCR2, SCTLR2, S1PIE and S1POE to protected VMs
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

Advertise FEAT_TCR2, FEAT_SCTLR2, FEAT_S1PIE and FEAT_S1POE to
protected VMs, with S1POE only when the kernel supports POE, as EL2
doesn't switch the POE registers otherwise. With the fields set,
vcpu_set_hcrx() sets HCRX_EL2.{TCR2En,SCTLR2En} for them, and since
the previous patch their PIE and POE registers don't trap.

Reset the new registers as KVM does for a non-protected vCPU.

Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

[PATCH v2 11/20] KVM: arm64: Compute a protected vCPU's fine-grained traps at EL2
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

pKVM sets a protected vCPU's HCR_EL2, MDCR_EL2 and HCRX_EL2 at EL2 from
the VM's ID registers, but never computes its fine-grained traps, which
stay 0. That traps every register with a negative-polarity trap bit, so
a feature such as S1PIE can't be advertised to a protected VM.

Link config.c into nVHE hyp and compute the VM's FGU masks and the
vCPU's fine-grained traps there, under vm_table_lock, once the ID
registers are set. Build hyp without the latent_entropy plugin, which
adds a reference to config.c's __init functions that hyp can't resolve.

This also restores the AmpereOne AC03_CPU_38 masking of a protected
vCPU's TCR_EL1 writes, which was lost when the erratum's trap moved
into the per-vCPU fine-grained traps.

Fixes: fb10ddf35c1cc ("KVM: arm64: Compute per-vCPU FGTs at vcpu_load()")
Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/hyp/nvhe/Makefile | 4 +++-
 arch/arm64/kvm/hyp/nvhe/pkvm.c   | 2 ++
 2 files changed, 5 insertions(+), 1 deletion(-)

[PATCH v2 10/20] KVM: arm64: Advertise the GICv3 CPU interface to protected VMs on GICv5
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

A GICv5 host with FEAT_GCIE_LEGACY runs GICv3 guests, but its own
ID_AA64PFR0_EL1.GIC is 0. KVM sets the field to IMP for non-protected
VMs on such a host, but not for protected VMs, which read it from EL2's
copy of the register. The next patch computes a protected vCPU's
fine-grained traps from its ID registers, and with GIC at 0, EL2 would
then inject an UNDEF for the guest's ICC_IGRPEN<n>_EL1 accesses.

Set the field to IMP in EL2's copy as well. The vgic isn't probed yet
when the copy is made, so test the capability the vgic itself tests.

Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

[PATCH v2 08/20] KVM: arm64: Move the VM's FGU initialisation into config.c
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

The first time one of a VM's vCPUs runs, kvm_calculate_traps() computes
the VM's fine-grained UNDEF masks from its ID registers, one
compute_fgu() call per FGT group, and sets KVM_ARCH_FLAG_FGU_INITIALIZED
so that it computes them once. The next patch computes the same masks
for protected VMs at EL2.

Move the calls and the flag into kvm_init_fgu() in config.c, so that a
group added later is computed at EL2 too. compute_fgu() then has no
caller outside config.c, so make it static.

No functional change intended.

Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

[PATCH v2 09/20] KVM: arm64: Report an incomplete feature map with WARN_ON()
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

At init, check_feature_map() reports with kvm_err() any feature map
that doesn't account for every bit of its register. The next patch
builds config.c into nVHE hyp, which has no printk(), so the check
would need an #ifdef to keep it out of hyp.

Use WARN_ON() instead, which doesn't need printk().

[PATCH v2 07/20] KVM: arm64: Allow protected VMs to use SVE
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

Now that EL2 owns a protected vCPU's SVE state and advertises
ID_AA64ZFR0_EL1, allow KVM_CAP_ARM_SVE for a protected VM, which lets
KVM_ARM_VCPU_INIT take KVM_ARM_VCPU_SVE.

A protected guest's SVE access trap injects an UNDEF. An SVE
instruction takes the SVE trap rather than the FP one, so a guest whose
first vector instruction is an SVE one would get an UNDEF instead of
the lazy FP switch. Handle the SVE trap as the FP trap is:
kvm_handle_pvm_fpsimd() switches the state for a vCPU with SVE and
still injects the UNDEF for one without.

Update the documentation to match.

Tested-by: Vincent Donnefort <vdonnefort@google.com>
Reviewed-by: Vincent Donnefort <vdonnefort@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

[PATCH v2 06/20] KVM: arm64: Advertise ID_AA64ZFR0_EL1 to protected VMs
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

EL2 builds a protected guest's ID registers from the host's sanitised
values, most of them capped per field. It has no case for
ID_AA64ZFR0_EL1, so the register reads as 0, and a protected guest with
SVE wouldn't see any of the SVE extensions the CPUs implement.

Keep a copy of the host's sanitised ID_AA64ZFR0_EL1 at EL2, and
advertise it when the guest has SVE, as KVM does for a non-protected
guest. Cap each field at the highest value the sysreg file defines.

Tested-by: Vincent Donnefort <vdonnefort@google.com>
Reviewed-by: Vincent Donnefort <vdonnefort@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

[PATCH v2 05/20] KVM: arm64: Allocate a protected vCPU's SVE state at EL2
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

For a vCPU with SVE, EL2 pins the buffer the host allocated at
KVM_ARM_VCPU_FINALIZE and saves the guest's SVE registers there. The
host can still read and write that buffer, so it can't hold a protected
vCPU's state.

Allocate a protected vCPU's SVE state from the hyp heap instead, and
free it with the vCPU. When EL2 resets a protected vCPU, zero its SVE
state and ZCR_EL1, as KVM does for a non-protected one. The host still
allocates its own buffer, which KVM_{GET,SET}_ONE_REG access before the
first run, but EL2 doesn't use it.

For a protected vCPU, the host's sve_max_vl now sizes an EL2 allocation
and is the guest's maximum vector length on every CPU. Give EL2 a copy
of kvm_sve_max_vl, the largest length all CPUs can give a guest, and
check every vCPU's sve_max_vl against it rather than clamping it to the
host's own maximum. set_sve_vls() already keeps the host's value within
that bound.

Tested-by: Vincent Donnefort <vdonnefort@google.com>
Reviewed-by: Vincent Donnefort <vdonnefort@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
---

[PATCH v2 04/20] KVM: arm64: Remove redundant kern_hyp_va() calls from the pKVM FP sync
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

With pKVM, EL2 saves FP and SVE state through the hyp vCPU, whose kvm
is the hyp VM's and whose sve_state is already a hyp address.
kern_hyp_va() returns both unchanged.

Remove the calls in fpsimd_sve_sync() and __hyp_sve_save_guest(), and
the one in kvm_hyp_save_fpsimd_host(), which only pKVM reaches. The
ones in __hyp_sve_restore_guest() and kvm_hyp_handle_fpsimd() stay, as
nVHE without pKVM reaches them with the host's pointers.

No functional change intended.

[PATCH v2 03/20] KVM: arm64: Add support for self-hosted debug in protected guests
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

pKVM hides DebugVer from protected guests and makes their debug
registers RAZ/WI or UNDEFINED, so they don't have breakpoints,
watchpoints or single-step. For a non-protected guest, the host sets the
owner of the debug registers at vcpu load, from the guest's MDSCR_EL1
and OS Lock, and EL2 copies the owner and the registers in at each
entry. However, the host can't see a protected guest's registers.

Set the owner at EL2 for a protected vCPU instead, by the rules the host
uses for a non-protected guest, but keep MDCR_EL2.TDA set when the guest
is the owner. A trap handler returns to the guest without going back
through the world switch, so a handler that changes the owner switches
the debug registers itself. The PSCI CPU_ON reset runs after the vcpu
load, so it sets the owner again.

Advertise DebugVer, capped at V8P8 as for a non-protected guest, and the
breakpoint, watchpoint and context-aware breakpoint counts. The other
debug registers that took an UNDEF behave as they do for a non-protected
guest that doesn't own its debug registers.

Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
---

[PATCH v2 02/20] KVM: arm64: Don't advertise the host's MTE_frac to protected VMs
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

Protected VMs don't support MTE, so MTE_frac in their ID_AA64PFR1_EL1
doesn't apply and is RES0, as it is for a non-protected VM without MTE.

Commit 3d7ff00700d1a ("KVM: arm64: Rework specifying restricted
features for protected VMs") caps the field at NI in the pVM
restriction table. However, NI is 0xF and the clamp is unsigned, so the
cap does nothing and a protected guest sees the host's value. On a CPU
with MTE2 but without FEAT_MTE_ASYNC, that value is 0xF.

Drop the entry so that the field reads as 0 again. It was the only
entry capping an unsigned field at an NI other than 0.

Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/hyp/nvhe/sys_regs.c | 1 -
 1 file changed, 1 deletion(-)

[PATCH v2 01/20] KVM: arm64: Only allow ptrauth for a pVM on a system with full ptrauth
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

EL2 allows a protected VM both ptrauth features whatever the system
supports. An id_aa64isar1 override can leave a system with generic but
not address auth. EL2 then doesn't trap the guest's key accesses, but
it doesn't switch the keys either, since the switch is gated on
ARM64_HAS_ADDRESS_AUTH. The host can read and write the guest's
APGAKey.

Only allow the ptrauth features when the system has full ptrauth, as
the host does.

Fixes: b56680de9c648 ("KVM: arm64: Initialize trap register values in hyp in pKVM")
Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

[PATCH] KVM: arm64: selftests: Pin vpmu_counter_access to one CPU
Author: James Clark <james.clark@linaro.org>

The vpmu_counter_access test reads the maximum PMCR_EL0.N from one VM
and then creates new VMs for each number of counters up to that limit.
If the test migrates between CPU types the limit may not be valid for
the new VM which causes:

  ==== Test Assertion Failure ====
    arm64/vpmu_counter_access.c:483: !ret
    pid=9165 tid=9165 errno=22 - Invalid argument
  ...
    KVM_SET_DEVICE_ATTR failed, rc: -1 errno: 22 (Invalid argument)

For example on a Radxa Orion O6 with 20 counters on the big cores and 6
on the little cores, this happens on most runs.

Fix it by pinning the test to the CPU that it starts on before any VMs
are created.

Signed-off-by: James Clark <james.clark@linaro.org>
---
Assisted by Claude Opus 5.5.
---

[PATCH 2/2] KVM: s390: selftests: Add a test for nested guests in ESA mode
Author: Yvan Janssens <yvanj@cyber.gent>

Run a nested guest from a state description without the z/Architecture
flag, with KVM_CAP_S390_VSIE_ESAMODE enabled, and let it execute STORE
FACILITY LIST EXTENDED twice. vsie intercepts the first one of every SIE
entry to shadow the facility list guest 2 designates and then has the
instruction repeated.

The test checks that the second doubleword of the PSW field, which is not
part of an ESA/390 PSW, comes back unchanged, that the PSW points behind
the last instruction, and that the first instruction stored the
designated list. What the second instruction stored is printed for
diagnosis.

The test is skipped without the capability, without interpretive
execution for the guest (SIEF2) and without facility 7 in the guest's
model.

Assisted-by: Claude:claude-fable-5-2
Signed-off-by: Yvan Janssens <yvanj@cyber.gent>

[PATCH 1/2] KVM: s390: vsie: Rewind the PSW of ESA mode guests in the right format
Author: Yvan Janssens <yvanj@cyber.gent>

A state description without the z/Architecture flag holds the eight-byte
ESA/390 PSW in the first doubleword of its PSW field: the addressing mode
is bit 32 and the instruction address is in bits 33-63 of that doubleword
(Principles of Operation, "Program-Status Word" as it applies in the
ESA/390-compatibility mode). The second doubleword is not part of the
PSW.

Since nested guests can be run in ESA mode, vsie still rewinds the PSW of
their shadow state description as if it were a z/Architecture PSW. It
subtracts the instruction length from the second doubleword, which leaves
the instruction address alone and stores a wrapped address there
(0x7ffffffc for one four-byte instruction). The value is copied back into
the state description of guest 2 when the shadow is released.

The rewind is how vsie repeats an instruction: STORE FACILITY LIST
EXTENDED after the facility list that guest 2 designates has been
shadowed (handle_stfle()), MOVE PAGE after a fault was resolved
(vsie_handle_mvpg()), and the instruction that is reported with an
addressing exception when guest 3 touches memory guest 2 does not have.
For a guest in ESA mode the instruction is skipped instead. The first
STORE FACILITY LIST EXTENDED after every entry into SIE returns with its
operand and the condition code untouched, so a program in the nested
guest that looks at the facility list sees zeros.

Rewind the instruction address where the format of the state description
has it.

The selftest in the next patch reproduces the problem: on 7.3.6 the
second doubleword of the PSW field comes back as 0x7ffffff8 after two
skipped instructions and the nested guest never stores a facility list.

Fixes: b0ad874d9852 ("KVM: s390: vsie: Allow non-zarch guests")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org # 7.1+
Assisted-by: Claude:claude-fable-5-2
Signed-off-by: Yvan Janssens <yvanj@cyber.gent>
---
 arch/s390/kvm/s390/vsie.c | 23 +++++++++++++++++++++--
 1 file changed, 21 insertions(+), 2 deletions(-)

----------------------------------------------------------------------

[PATCH 0/2] KVM: s390: vsie: fix instruction retry for nested guests in ESA mode
Author: Yvan Janssens <yvanj@cyber.gent>

Since v7.1 a nested hypervisor can run its guests in ESA mode (the
ESA/390-compatibility mode of the machine) when user space enables
KVM_CAP_S390_VSIE_ESAMODE. One piece of vsie still treats the PSW of such a
guest as a z/Architecture PSW: the rewind that vsie uses to have an
instruction repeated. For a state description in ESA/390 format the
instruction is skipped instead, and a stray value is left in the half of
the PSW field that such a state description does not use.

What a nested guest sees: the first STORE FACILITY LIST EXTENDED after
every entry into SIE does nothing (operand and condition code untouched),
because vsie intercepts it to shadow the facility list. A program that
tests facility bits gets zeros.

Patch 1 is the fix, patch 2 a selftest that reproduces the problem with a
small guest 2 that issues SIE itself (modelled on the vsie selftest that is
queued in kvm-s390 next). The series is based on v7.4-rc4-260-g4dce97c6c265.

Why it matters beyond one instruction: backward compatibility is a
defining property of this platform, and the ESA/390-compatibility mode is
the architecture's mechanism for running older programs on machines that
only start in z/Architecture mode. Any hypervisor that runs as a KVM guest
and gives its own guests that mode depends on vsie handling such state
descriptions exactly like the machine.

Testing (z14)
- On an LPAR running v7.3.6 with patch 1 (the host owns SIE) the selftest
  of patch 2 passes all three checks: the unused doubleword stays 0, the
  PSW ends behind the last instruction, and the first STFLE stores the
  designated list.
- Without patch 1 (v7.3.6, test kernel running as a KVM guest above a level
  that passed ESA mode state descriptions on): the unused PSW doubleword
  comes back as 0x7ffffff8 and neither STFLE stores anything. In such a
  nested setup above a host without the fix, checks 1 and 3 still fail
  with patch 1 in the test kernel, because the defect acts once per level.
- On a host whose lower level refuses ESA mode state descriptions the test
  skips (validity 0x0001 seen by guest 2).
- KVM selftests (12 s390 tests and 5 generic ones) and the s390x
  kvm-unit-tests (37 pass, 4 fail, 21 skip) give the same result for every
  test with and without the series (v7.3.6, nested setup); the 4 failures
  (sclp-1g, sclp-3g, css, edat) are the same with the unmodified module.
- Built with W=1 and sparse without warnings; checkpatch --strict is clean
  apart from the MAINTAINERS note for the new file.
- Not tested: MOVE PAGE partial execution and the addressing-exception
  path for a guest in ESA mode. MOVE PAGE is not available in the mode
  without the ESA/XC facilities, so the first should not occur.

Assistance: the series was prepared with an AI coding assistant (see the
Assisted-by tags); the defect was found while tracing why a facility test
failed in a nested guest, the analysis, fix and test were written with the
assistant and reviewed by the submitter.

Yvan Janssens (2):
  KVM: s390: vsie: Rewind the PSW of ESA mode guests in the right format
  KVM: s390: selftests: Add a test for nested guests in ESA mode

[PATCH kvmtool v6 00/23] arm64: Support GICv5-based guests
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

This series adds support for GICv5-based VMs to kvmtool. These are
exposed as --irqchip=gicv5 and --irqchip=gicv5-its, where the former
creates a vGICv5 device and the latter adds a GICv5 ITS. The GICv5
specification can be found at [1].

The GICv5 support in KVM needed by --irqchip=gicv5 is available as of
Linux v7.4-rc1. The GICv5 ITS support is described in [2]; using
--irqchip=gicv5-its requires a KVM build with those changes.

The series teaches kvmtool to create the GICv5 irqchip and ITS KVM
devices. It describes the GICv5 CPU interface, IRS and ITS in the
guest DT, updates the timer and PMU interrupt descriptions, and
generates GICv5 interrupt specifiers for platform devices and PCI. It
also maps PCI MSI requester IDs to the ITS. The IRS support is built
and exposed before the ITS support, so the GICv5 irqchip support can
be merged separately from the ITS changes.

Compared with v5 [3]:

* Dropped the SETLPI frame from the IRS DT description because KVM
  reserves but does not emulate it. Updated patch 15's commit message
  accordingly.

* Added Vladimir's Reviewed-by trailers to patches 9 and 15.

The v7.4-rc1 header sync includes GICv5 IRS UAPI, but not GICv5 ITS
UAPI. Patch 19 mirrors the ITS definitions from [2]. Once that UAPI
lands upstream, the headers should be resynced against the latest
Linux headers before merging the ITS support.

Thanks,
Sascha

[1] https://developer.arm.com/documentation/aes0070/latest
[2] https://lore.kernel.org/all/20261102194454.4086911-1-sascha.bischoff@arm.com/
[3] https://lore.kernel.org/all/20261118175755.1946977-1-sascha.bischoff@arm.com/

[PATCH] RISC-V: KVM: Add missing write barrier when linking G-stage page tables
Author: Tan Chi <tanchi25@mails.ucas.ac.cn>

kvm_riscv_gstage_set_pte() links a newly allocated page table
to its parent PTE without a write memory barrier.

A concurrent hardware page table walker may observe the parent
PTE before the child page table is fully initialized, potentially
interpreting stale contents as valid PTEs.

Add an smp_wmb() before linking the child page table, as is
already done in kvm_riscv_gstage_split_huge().

Sashiko reported this pre-existing issue while reviewing my
G-stage page table accounting patch. LLM assistance was used
to analyze the issue and prepare the changelog.

Fixes: 9d05c1fee837 ("RISC-V: KVM: Implement stage2 page table programming")
Link: https://sashiko.dev/#/bug/linux-9339e577-5f31-42a2-a5ea-9844d30b43f8

Signed-off-by: Tan Chi <tanchi25@mails.ucas.ac.cn>
---
 arch/riscv/kvm/gstage.c | 7 +++++++
 1 file changed, 7 insertions(+)

[PATCH v4 2/2] virt: tdx-guest: Poll Quote status every 10 ms
Author: Moe Mahhouk via B4 Relay <devnull+moe.flashbots.net@kernel.org>


wait_for_quote_completion() checks the status of a GetQuote request
once and then sleeps for a full second before each further check. The
VMM has rarely completed the request at the first check, so every Quote
request takes at least one second, however quickly the host answers.

With a local Quote Generation Service the VMM completes the request in
a few milliseconds. On an Emerald Rapids host (KVM, QEMU 11.1, Intel
QGS), back-to-back Quote requests through configfs-tsm took 1021-1024 ms
each; with this change they take 14-21 ms (median 16 ms) at HZ=250.
Since quote_lock serializes requests, the one-second floor also caps a
TD at about one Quote per second, which hurts users that bind a fresh
Quote to every connection, such as attested TLS.

Recheck the status every 10 ms instead. msleep_interruptible() rounds
up to whole jiffies and adds one, so at HZ=250 a step takes 16 ms. The
sleep stays interruptible, and the timeout becomes a deadline in
jiffies.

Suggested-by: Rick Edgecombe <rick.p.edgecombe@intel.com>
Signed-off-by: Moe Mahhouk <moe@flashbots.net>
---
 drivers/virt/coco/tdx-guest/tdx-guest.c | 16 ++++++++++------
 1 file changed, 10 insertions(+), 6 deletions(-)

----------------------------------------------------------------------

[PATCH v4 0/2] virt: tdx-guest: Fix and speed up the wait for a
Author: Moe Mahhouk via B4 Relay <devnull+moe.flashbots.net@kernel.org>

Getting a TDX Quote takes at least one second, because
wait_for_quote_completion() sleeps a full second before each status
check, while a local Quote Generation Service answers in a few
milliseconds. Patch 2 rechecks the status every 10 ms instead.

In v1, Kiryl noticed that the same loop gets a real timeout wrong.
Patch 1 fixes that on its own, so that it can be backported; patch 2
then replaces the loop.

The GHCI SetupEventNotifyInterrupt mechanism would avoid polling
altogether, but it relies on the VMM implementing that service. This
series keeps the existing polling model, which works with any VMM.

Jun Miao posted a patch for the same latency in September [1]. It polls
every 2 ms with read_poll_timeout() and lowers the timeout to 1 s. This
series keeps the 30 s timeout. Patch 1 is needed for released kernels
either way.

[1] https://lore.kernel.org/all/20260917102202.750895-1-jun.miao@intel.com/

Testing on mainline 7be4bc52fd9e in a TDX guest (KVM host v7.0, QEMU
11.1.2, TDX module 1.5.34, Intel QGS, guest HZ=250), through
configfs-tsm:
- Latency, 120 back-to-back Quotes: 1021-1024 ms each before (v3
  numbers, same setup), 14-20 ms (median 16 ms) with the series. At
  HZ=250, msleep_interruptible(10) sleeps 4 jiffies, 16 ms, and the
  first check right after the request almost never succeeds.
- Stress, 1000 back-to-back Quotes: none empty, 14-21 ms (median
  16 ms).
- Timeout, with a QGS socket that accepts and never answers: the read
  fails with -ETIMEDOUT after 30 s. Patch 1 is unchanged from v3, where
  the lowered-timeout test showed the fix.
- SIGTERM to a reader waiting on such a request: it exits within the
  same 10 ms tick of /proc/uptime.
- W=1 build of drivers/virt/coco/tdx-guest/ and checkpatch.pl --strict
  are clean.

The patches and this cover letter were written with an AI coding
assistant, Claude (Claude Code, model Claude Opus 5.5), which also ran
the tests.

---
Changes in v4:
- Patch 2: recheck every 10 ms instead of backing off from 1 to 100 ms
  (Rick). Title changed to "Poll Quote status every 10 ms"
- Patch 2: changelog says the first check comes before the first sleep
  (Xiaoyao)
- Patch 2: dropped Kiryl's and Sathyanarayanan's Reviewed-by, since the
  code changed
- Patch 1: collected Reviewed-by (Xiaoyao)
- Link to v3: https://patch.msgid.link/20261024-tdx-guest-quote-poll-v3-0-6a83bb767314@flashbots.net

---
Moe Mahhouk (2):
      virt: tdx-guest: Fix timeout detection in wait_for_quote_completion()
      virt: tdx-guest: Poll Quote status every 10 ms

[PATCH v4 1/2] virt: tdx-guest: Fix timeout detection in
Author: Moe Mahhouk via B4 Relay <devnull+moe.flashbots.net@kernel.org>


wait_for_quote_completion() counts the seconds it has waited in i and
reports a timeout when i == timeout. That gets both outcomes of the
last second wrong:

- If the request is still in flight after the last sleep, the loop
  condition increments i once more before it fails, so the loop ends
  with i == timeout + 1 and the function returns 0. The caller then
  returns -EIO instead of -ETIMEDOUT, without the "GetQuote request
  timedout" message. Before commit 0f409eaea53e ("virt: tdx-guest:
  Return error for GetQuote failures") a timed-out request even
  returned success with an empty Quote.

- If the VMM completes the request during the last sleep, the loop ends
  on the status check with i == timeout, and the function returns
  -ETIMEDOUT for a completed request, discarding the Quote.

Decide on the Quote buffer instead: the request timed out if it is
still in flight when the wait ends.

Fixes: f4738f56d1dc ("virt: tdx-guest: Add Quote generation support using TSM_REPORTS")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Reported-by: Kiryl Shutsemau <kas@kernel.org>
Closes: https://lore.kernel.org/all/atoHWgQF-0vlTRK4@thinkstation/
Reviewed-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>
Reviewed-by: Kuppuswamy Sathyanarayanan <sathyanarayanan.kuppuswamy@linux.intel.com>
Reviewed-by: Xiaoyao Li <xiaoyao.li@intel.com>
Signed-off-by: Moe Mahhouk <moe@flashbots.net>
---
 drivers/virt/coco/tdx-guest/tdx-guest.c | 2 +-
 1 file changed, 1 insertion(+), 1 deletion(-)

[PATCH] KVM: nVMX: Expire the emulated preemption timer immediately if its deadline has passed
Author: Amirmohammad Eftekhar <amirmohammad.eftekhar@cispa.de>

vmx_calc_preemption_timer_value() returns the unclamped u64
"preemption_timer_deadline - l1_scaled_tsc".  The deadline is reused, not
recomputed from vmcs12, on the two entries to L2 that are not
VMLAUNCH/VMRESUME: RSM back into L2 (has_preemption_timer_deadline
survives the SMI) and KVM_SET_NESTED_STATE with
KVM_STATE_VMX_PREEMPTION_TIMER_DEADLINE.  If the deadline has already
passed by then, the subtraction wraps to ~2^64 and
vmx_start_preemption_timer() arms the hrtimer ~2^64 / virtual_tsc_khz ns
in the future (about two hours at 2.6 GHz) instead of synthesizing the
immediate preemption-timer VM-Exit.  L2 then keeps running without the
exit L1 asked for until some other event forces a VM-Exit.

Reachable by:
 - an SMI taken in L2 whose SMM handler runs longer than the remaining
   timer (SDM Vol. 3C 27.5.1: the timer-induced VM exit occurs
   immediately after RSM if the timer expired during SMI, in SMM, or
   during RSM);
 - a guest WRMSR to IA32_TSC / IA32_TSC_ADJUST from SMM that moves L1's
   TSC past the deadline (the timer counts only TSC increments, so a TSC
   write must not affect it at all);
 - KVM_SET_NESTED_STATE with a restored deadline that has already passed,
   e.g. a live migration whose blackout spans the expiry.

The original deadline code, 850448f35aaf ("KVM: nVMX: Fix VMX preemption
timer migration"), returned 0 in this case; the guard was dropped by the
nested.c hunk of the follow-up commit.

Fixes: 8d7fbf01f9af ("KVM: selftests: VMX preemption timer migration test")
Signed-off-by: Amirmohammad Eftekhar <amirmohammad.eftekhar@cispa.de>
---
Observed inside a disposable nested L1 (KVM as both L0 and the L1 under
test) on mainline and on 6.1.74, exercising all three routes.  With a
deadline set just in the past, the L2 preemption-timer hrtimer is armed
about 7064 s out (2^64 / 2611200 kHz) instead of causing the immediate
exit, and L2 runs for the whole observation window with no exit; the
controls (no deadline, future deadline, SMI with a short handler) exit on
time.  No warning, BUG or KASAN on either kernel.

 arch/x86/kvm/vmx/nested.c | 12 ++++++++++++
 1 file changed, 12 insertions(+)

[PATCH] RISC-V: KVM: Account G-stage page table memory
Author: Tan Chi <tanchi25@mails.ucas.ac.cn>

RISC-V KVM currently does not account G-stage page table memory
in NR_SECONDARY_PAGETABLE.

Use kvm_account_pgtable_pages() to account for the allocation and
freeing of the root and lower-level page tables, including those
allocated when splitting huge page mappings.

This makes G-stage page table memory usage visible through
SecPageTables in /proc/meminfo and sec_pagetables in the cgroup
memory.stat interface.

Update the documentation accordingly.

Tested on a RISC-V KVM host running under QEMU TCG with Sv57x4.
The observed G-stage page table memory usage was:

  100 empty VMs:                         1600 KiB
  8 GiB sparsely mapped with 4 KiB pages: 16448 KiB
  256 MiB mapped with 2 MiB pages:          36 KiB
  After enabling dirty logging:           548 KiB

The accounting in /proc/meminfo, /proc/vmstat and cgroup
memory.stat matched the expected values and returned to zero
after destroying the VMs.

This reimplements the work originally proposed by Yosry Ahmed
in 2022, adapted to the current G-stage MMU code.

Link: https://lore.kernel.org/r/20220426053904.3684293-6-yosryahmed@google.com

Signed-off-by: Tan Chi <tanchi25@mails.ucas.ac.cn>
---

[PATCH v10 18/18] PCI/TSM: Wait for vIOMMUs to release the TSM before unregistering
Author: Aneesh Kumar K.V (Arm) <aneesh.kumar@kernel.org>

An IOMMUFD virtual IOMMU (vIOMMU) implemented by a TEE Security Manager
(TSM) uses the TSM driver's resources throughout its lifetime, including
during destruction.

A reference to tsm_dev keeps that structure allocated. Pinning the TSM
driver module keeps its code loaded. Neither prevents an explicit
tsm_unregister() call from disconnecting devices and removing driver
resources while the vIOMMU still needs them.

Add a TSM user count for references acquired through tsm_get_device()
and released through tsm_put_device(). Reject new references once
unregister starts, then wait until the vIOMMUs using the TSM have
finished destruction and released their references before removing the
driver's resources.

Also coordinate removal of physical function 0 (PF0), which hosts the
PCI Device Security Manager (DSM), with TSM unregister. PF0 removal
waits for DSM users on a completion stored inside PF0. Hold
registration_lock for read through that wait and removal, so unregister
cannot free PF0 underneath it.

If unregister already holds the write lock, wait for all TSM users to
release their references instead of waiting for dsm_context_users to
reach zero. Use the completion stored in tsm_dev, and retain a tsm_dev
reference throughout the wait so that completion cannot be freed.

Cc: Ashish Kalra <ashish.kalra@amd.com>
Cc: Tom Lendacky <thomas.lendacky@amd.com>
Cc: Herbert Xu <herbert@gondor.apana.org.au>
Cc: Alexey Kardashevskiy <aik@amd.com>
Cc: Jason Gunthorpe <jgg@ziepe.ca>
Cc: Lukas Wunner <lukas@wunner.de>
Cc: Bjorn Helgaas <bhelgaas@google.com>
Cc: Xu Yilun <yilun.xu@linux.intel.com>
Cc: Kevin Tian <kevin.tian@intel.com>
Cc: linux-pci@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Aneesh Kumar K.V (Arm) <aneesh.kumar@kernel.org>
---

[PATCH v10 17/18] PCI/TSM: Wait for DSM users before removing PF0
Author: Aneesh Kumar K.V (Arm) <aneesh.kumar@kernel.org>

Removing physical function 0 (PF0) attempts to disconnect its TSM link.
If a sibling function still has an IOMMUFD vDEVICE, dsm_context_users is
nonzero and disconnect returns -EBUSY. Removal currently continues to
pci_doe_destroy(), destroying PF0's Data Object Exchange (DOE) mailbox
while the assigned function may still need it.

Reject new DSM users and subfunction attachments once PF0 removal
starts. Wait for dsm_context_users to reach zero before disconnecting
the link and allowing PCI removal to destroy the mailbox.

Release pci_tsm_rwsem while waiting, since vDEVICE cleanup needs its
read side to decrement the count. Reacquire it for write before
disconnecting.

Cc: Bjorn Helgaas <bhelgaas@google.com>
Cc: Jonathan Cameron <jonathan.cameron@huawei.com>
Cc: Alexey Kardashevskiy <aik@amd.com>
Cc: Xu Yilun <yilun.xu@linux.intel.com>
Cc: Lukas Wunner <lukas@wunner.de>
Cc: Samuel Ortiz <sameo@rivosinc.com>
Cc: Suzuki K Poulose <suzuki.poulose@arm.com>

[PATCH v10 16/18] iommufd/viommu: Allow vIOMMUs without a parent HWPT
Author: Aneesh Kumar K.V (Arm) <aneesh.kumar@kernel.org>

VIOMMU_ALLOC currently requires a nesting parent HWPT_PAGING even when
the selected vIOMMU implementation has no use for one. This would force
implementations such as the following Arm Realm vIOMMU to create an
unused parent.

Add IOMMUFD_VIOMMU_NO_HWPT so an implementation can opt out. Require
hwpt_id to be zero in that case and pass a NULL parent domain to its
initialization callback. For implementations requiring a parent, verify
that it supports nesting and belongs to the selected physical IOMMU
driver before invoking viommu_init().

Reject nested domain and hardware queue allocation for a vIOMMU without
a parent. Nested domain allocation also requires physical IOMMU backing.
Document the two allocation modes in the UAPI.

[PATCH v10 14/18] iommufd/viommu: Identify physical IOMMU backing with a tag
Author: Aneesh Kumar K.V (Arm) <aneesh.kumar@kernel.org>

When allocating a vDEVICE or nested HWPT, IOMMUFD checks that the device
and its vIOMMU use the same physical IOMMU.

The next patch adds vIOMMUs implemented by a TSM. These store a TSM
pointer instead of a physical IOMMU pointer, so the same comparison
cannot be used.

Add a backing field to identify vIOMMUs that use a physical IOMMU, and
perform the comparison only for those vIOMMUs. All existing vIOMMUs
select that value, so their behavior is unchanged.

Signed-off-by: Aneesh Kumar K.V (Arm) <aneesh.kumar@kernel.org>

[PATCH v10 13/18] PCI/TSM: Add helpers to look up and reference a device's TSM
Author: Aneesh Kumar K.V (Arm) <aneesh.kumar@kernel.org>

Add tsm_get_device() to find and reference the TSM attached to a device,
using pci_tsm_get_device() for PCI devices. Resolve the PCI association
under pci_tsm_rwsem and acquire TSM device and backend module
references. Return NULL when no TSM is attached and an error if the
module cannot be pinned.

Add tsm_put_device() to release the device reference before the module
reference, since the final device put may call into the backend module.

Set the AMD SEV TSM operations owner to THIS_MODULE so lookup pins the
module implementing its callbacks.

Following patch lets IOMMUFD use vIOMMU operations implemented by the
selected device's TSM. vIOMMU allocation must look up that TSM to find
its operations. The vIOMMU keeps the TSM device and module references
until it is destroyed, so the TSM allocation cannot be freed and its
callback code cannot be unloaded while the vIOMMU can still call it.

Signed-off-by: Aneesh Kumar K.V (Arm) <aneesh.kumar@kernel.org>

[PATCH v10 12/18] iommufd/viommu: Select vIOMMU operations before allocation
Author: Aneesh Kumar K.V (Arm) <aneesh.kumar@kernel.org>

Physical IOMMU drivers currently provide the vIOMMU size and
initialization callbacks through iommu_ops. The initialization callback
then selects and installs the corresponding iommufd_viommu_ops.

Add iommu_ops::get_viommu_ops() to select the operations from the
physical device and requested vIOMMU type before allocation. Move the
size and initialization callbacks into iommufd_viommu_ops. The core can
then validate the selected operations, allocate the driver structure,
initialize it, and install the operations.

Convert AMD, Arm SMMUv3, Tegra CMDQV, and the selftest backend to the
new interface.

The current code requires a valid parent HWPT before calling
viommu_init(), which selects the vIOMMU operations. A later patch adds
an operations flag indicating that a vIOMMU type does not require a
parent HWPT. Selecting the operations earlier allows the core to check
that flag before deciding whether to look up and validate a parent HWPT.

Temporarily disable AMD vIOMMU allocation by returning NULL from
amd_iommufd_get_viommu_ops() while the vIOMMU interfaces are being
updated.

[PATCH v10 10/18] PCI/TSM: Add helpers to prevent disconnect while in use
Author: Aneesh Kumar K.V (Arm) <aneesh.kumar@kernel.org>

Add PCI TEE Security Manager (TSM) helpers that prevent PF0 disconnect
while a function is in use. Validate the function's TSM association and
increment PF0's dsm_context_users count on get. Find PF0 through the
same function and decrement the count on put. Reject disconnect with
-EBUSY until all users have released it.

The caller must prevent removal of the function between get and put, for
example by keeping its VFIO device open. No per-call allocation is
needed.

Cc: Bjorn Helgaas <bhelgaas@google.com>

[PATCH v10 08/18] iommufd: Add a vDEVICE operation ioctl framework
Author: Aneesh Kumar K.V (Arm) <aneesh.kumar@kernel.org>

Add IOMMU_VDEVICE_OP to execute operations on a vDEVICE using separate
request and response buffers. Each operation defines its request and
response formats, with response capacity checked before changing device
state.

Look up the vDEVICE and check its supported_ops mask before dispatching
an operation. Reject out-of-range operation numbers before testing the
corresponding bit. Backends set the mask during vdevice_init().

This patch defines no operations. The default dispatch returns
-EOPNOTSUPP; later patches can add operations independently.

[PATCH v10 06/18] iommufd/device: Associate KVM file pointer with iommufd_device
Author: Aneesh Kumar K.V (Arm) <aneesh.kumar@kernel.org>


TSM vDevice support needs access to the KVM associated with a VFIO device
after the device has been bound to iommufd.

Extend iommufd_device_bind() to accept the device's KVM file and store it

[PATCH v10 04/18] virt: coco: tsm: Complete PCI registration before publishing the device
Author: Aneesh Kumar K.V (Arm) <aneesh.kumar@kernel.org>

tsm_register() calls device_add() before initializing and validating the
PCI operations. This makes the new TSM discoverable through the class
lookup while PCI registration can still fail.

An existing link TSM can keep the PCI connect attribute visible during
registration of another TSM. A concurrent write naming the new TSM can
therefore reach it through:

  connect_store()
    find_tsm_dev()
      class_find_device()
    is_link_tsm()
    pci_tsm_connect()

If the new backend supplies both link and device security operations,
pci_tsm_register() rejects it before taking pci_tsm_rwsem. The registration
thread then follows:

  tsm_register()
    tsm_register_pci_or_reset()
      pci_tsm_register() -> -EINVAL
    device_del()
    put_tsm_dev()

If connect has already attached a PCI context, registration cleanup can
free a TSM still referenced by that context.

Complete PCI registration before device_add() publishes the class device.
On PCI registration failure, the TSM remains undiscoverable.

Send the PCI change notification only after device_add() succeeds.

Cc: Alexey Kardashevskiy <aik@amd.com>
Cc: Jason Gunthorpe <jgg@ziepe.ca>
Cc: Lukas Wunner <lukas@wunner.de>
Cc: linux-coco@lists.linux.dev
Fixes: 3225f52cde56 ("PCI/TSM: Establish Secure Sessions and Link Encryption")
Signed-off-by: Aneesh Kumar K.V (Arm) <aneesh.kumar@kernel.org>
---
 drivers/virt/coco/tsm-core.c | 34 ++++++++++++----------------------
 1 file changed, 12 insertions(+), 22 deletions(-)

[PATCH v10 03/18] virt: coco: tsm: Remove the device from lookup before PCI teardown
Author: Aneesh Kumar K.V (Arm) <aneesh.kumar@kernel.org>

PCI connect looks up a TSM by ID under pci_tsm_rwsem. Previously,
pci_tsm_unregister() released that lock after tearing down PCI state
while the TSM was still in the class lookup. A racing connect could then
attach a new PCI context that the teardown would never see.

Remove the device from the class lookup before pci_tsm_unregister() that
destroy their contexts. Drop the device reference only after PCI
teardown completes.

[PATCH v10 00/18] iommufd: TSM-backed virtual IOMMUs for confidential guests
Author: Aneesh Kumar K.V (Arm) <aneesh.kumar@kernel.org>

This series adds IOMMUFD and PCI/TSM infrastructure for assigning PCI
devices to confidential guests. A TEE Security Manager (TSM) can provide a
virtual IOMMU (vIOMMU), and an IOMMUFD vDEVICE object represents a physical
device within that virtual IOMMU.

The series adds a common framework for operations on vDEVICE objects and
updates lifetime management to keep the VM and device-security resources
available while those objects are in use.

The Arm CCA backend is provided in a separate series, together with
operations for TEE Device Interface Security Protocol (TDISP) state changes
and access to cached device-attestation objects.

The full series, including the Arm CCA backend, is available at:

  https://git.gitlab.arm.com/linux-arm/linux-cca.git 
  branch: cca/topics/7.4/cca-da-integration-v2

Notes:
* Codex was used to assist with commit message formatting and code
  rearrangement.

https://lore.kernel.org/all/20261116081935.85372-1-aneesh.kumar@kernel.org
* Split the PCI/TSM get/put helpers from removal of the legacy bind/unbind
  interface.
* Split the TSM lookup/reference helpers from their IOMMUFD
  callers
* Replace allocated PCI/TSM contexts with get/put helpers that update the
  DSM user count on PF0. Remove unused context accessors and
  pci_tsm_is_configured().
* Add a tagged union for the physical IOMMU and TSM pointers in a vIOMMU.

[PATCH v2 8/8] KVM: arm64: selftests: Test that a vcpu reset
Author: Dan Carpenter <dan@pereq.eu>

Add a test to check that KVM_ARM_VCPU_INIT (which resets a vcpu) has
reset all the things it was supposed to.

The test dirties as much of the vcpu state as it can, resets the vcpu,
and compares every register in KVM_GET_REG_LIST with a freshly
initialised vcpu.  The are a few things which we must not clear such as
MPIDR_EL1, which userspace configures and the reset keeps, and the
CLIDR_EL1 and CCSIDR counters which keep counting.

The test runs with and without EL2.

Signed-off-by: Dan Carpenter <dan@pereq.eu>

[PATCH v2 6/8] KVM: arm64: nv: Don't deliver a pending SError to
Author: Dan Carpenter <dan@pereq.eu>

With nested virtualization, when we inject an SError that the guest
has masked, we set the VSE bit in ->hcr_el2 and the CPU delivers it once
the guest unmasks SErrors.  The problem is that the bit stays set when
we switch between L1 and L2, so an SError meant for L2 can be delivered
to L1, and the other way around.

When we switch, clear the VSE bit and set NESTED_SERROR_PENDING instead.

This matches what kvm_nested_sync_hwstate() does after every guest exit.
Then kvm_nested_flush_hwstate() injects the SError again and decides
where it should go in the new context.

Reported-by: Sashiko <sashiko-bot@kernel.org>
Closes: https://lore.kernel.org/all/sashiko-outbox-229065@kernel.org/
Fixes: 77ee70a07357 ("KVM: arm64: nv: Honor SError exception routing / masking")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Dan Carpenter <dan@pereq.eu>
---
v2: new patch
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/emulate-nested.c | 14 ++++++++++++++
 1 file changed, 14 insertions(+)

[PATCH v2 7/8] KVM: arm64: nv: Re-inject a stored SError after the
Author: Dan Carpenter <dan@pereq.eu>

KVM only has room for one pending exception or PC increment per vcpu.
If something is already pending and we try to add another through
kvm_pend_exception() or kvm_incr_pc(), it triggers a WARN().  If we add
an exception on top of an exception, the first one is lost.  If we add a
PC increment on top of an exception, the PC increment is lost and the
trapped instruction runs again.

Normally an exit handler records an exception or a PC increment, and
__kvm_adjust_pc() commits it before the next guest entry.  However, with
nested virtualization kvm_nested_sync_hwstate() can add an exception
before the exit handler runs.  It re-injects a stored SError after the
guest exits but before handle_exit(), which can pend the SError as an
exception.  The exit handler then records its own on top, for example an
HVC forwarded to the guest hypervisor, the FPAC for a failed ERETAA, or
the PC increment for a trapped instruction it has emulated.  This
triggers the WARN().

In this situation where we have two exceptions, the correct order is to
handle the exit first and then the SError.  Change the
kvm_nested_sync_hwstate() function to only store the SError.  Then
kvm_nested_flush_hwstate() uses __kvm_adjust_pc() to commit the exit
handler's exception or PC increment, re-injects the SError, and the
guest is entered.  The early re-injection was there so that a pending
SError wakes the vcpu from WFI, so kvm_arch_vcpu_runnable() now treats
a deliverable stored SError as a wakeup.

Fixes: 77ee70a07357 ("KVM: arm64: nv: Honor SError exception routing / masking")
Fixes: ce66109cec86 ("KVM: arm64: nv: Take "masked" aborts to EL2 when HCRX_EL2.TMEA is set")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Dan Carpenter <dan@pereq.eu>
---
v2: No change
---

 4 files changed, 14 insertions(+), 10 deletions(-)

[PATCH v2 5/8] KVM: arm64: nv: Replace a pending SError when
Author: Dan Carpenter <dan@pereq.eu>

The kvm_inject_serror_esr() function injects SErrors as the name
implies.  The bug is that it doesn't handle already pending errors
correctly in nested virtualization.

In vanilla KVM the kvm_inject_serror_esr() function always sets
the SError to pending.  However with nested KVM, it might set it
to pending or it might deliver the SError directly.  We can only
have one pending error at a time.  Imagine we enter the function
with an SError pending already.  In the current code if we decide
to pend the new error that's fine, everything works.  However if
we decide to deliver it directly then the pend flags should be
cleared but they are not.  So we deliver the SError directly and
then the SError that was pending on the next exit or entry.

Clear the pend flags unconditionally at the start of the function.

Reported-by: Sashiko <sashiko-bot@kernel.org>
Closes: https://lore.kernel.org/all/sashiko-outbox-229065@kernel.org/
Fixes: ce66109cec86 ("KVM: arm64: nv: Take "masked" aborts to EL2 when HCRX_EL2.TMEA is set")
Fixes: 77ee70a07357 ("KVM: arm64: nv: Honor SError exception routing / masking")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Dan Carpenter <dan@pereq.eu>
---
v2: New patch
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/inject_fault.c | 4 ++++
 1 file changed, 4 insertions(+)

[PATCH v2 4/8] KVM: arm64: nv: Discard nested SError in
Author: Dan Carpenter <dan@pereq.eu>

Vanilla KVM stores a pending SError in ->hcr_el2 but nested KVM stores
it in the NESTED_SERROR_PENDING flag.  Clear the flag for nested
SErrors when we reset the vcpu.

[PATCH v2 3/8] KVM: arm64: Discard a pending SError when resetting
Author: Dan Carpenter <dan@pereq.eu>

Resetting a vcpu with the KVM_ARM_VCPU_INIT ioctl keeps a pending
SError.  We used to rely on vcpu_reset_hcr() to clear the SError but
commit f1ff3fc5209a ("KVM: arm64: unify code to prepare traps") changed
it to only reset HCR_EL2 before the vcpu's very first KVM_RUN.

If we reboot a guest while there is a pending SError then KVM delivers
it to the freshly booted guest.

Clear HCR_EL2.VSE in kvm_reset_vcpu().

Fixes: f1ff3fc5209a ("KVM: arm64: unify code to prepare traps")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Dan Carpenter <dan@pereq.eu>
